Prime-free discs in imaginary quadratic fields
Tanmay Khale

Abstract
. Suppose K \mathchar 29003 is an imaginary quadratic field, and let N K \mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29003} denote the field norm in 𝒪 K \mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003} . 1.
Introduction

Suppose
\mathchar 29003
is a number field, and let
\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29003}
denote the field norm in
\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}
. Let
\mathchar 28994\delimiter 67273472\dc{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29042\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\{\dc{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 12346\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\dc{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 8704\dc{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 12604\mathchar 29042\}
. Let
\mathchar 28999_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785
denote the size of the largest “hole” in primes of norm at most
\mathchar 29016
. That is,

\displaystyle\mathchar 28999_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785

\displaystyle\mathchar 12349\max\{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720\mathchar 12346\text{there exists }\dc{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}\text{ such that }\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\dc{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29016

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ and }\mathchar 28994\delimiter 67273472\dc{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29042\delimiter 84054785\text{ contains no primes}\}\mathchar 314

By the prime ideal theorem (Theorem
3.1
below),
\mathchar 28999_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785
is at least
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\log(X)
(where
\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785
denotes a function depending on
\mathchar 29003
which tends to zero as
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12833\mathchar 561
). For
\mathchar 29003\mathchar 12349\mathbb{\mathchar 29009}
, Westzynthius, Erdős and Rankin successively improved the lower bound above, showing for a
fixed
constant
\mathchar 29027\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720
, and writing
\log_{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785
to denote the
\mathchar 29035
-fold iterated logarithm, that

\mathchar 28999_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29009}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12821\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29027\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29039\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 67273472\log\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785{{\log_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\log_{\mathchar 28724}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\over\delimiter 67273472\log_{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28722}}}\mathchar 314

The above stood as the best-known result for 76 years, until in 2014 two papers
[
4
,
11
]
independently proved that the constant
\mathchar 29027
above could be taken to be
arbitrarily
large. In a subsequent collaboration
[
3
]
, the authors of the two papers showed that

(1.1)

\mathchar 28999_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29009}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12829\delimiter 67273472\log\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785{{\log_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\log_{\mathchar 28724}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\over\log_{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785}}\mathchar 314

In the more general case where
\mathchar 29003
is any imaginary quadratic field, the trivial lower bound
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\log(X)
has not previously been improved. The objective of this paper is to prove a lower bound generalizing (
1.1
) to any imaginary quadratic field
\mathchar 29003
. Our main result is the following:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\mathchar 29003
be an imaginary quadratic field. Then, we have

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathchar 28999_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12829_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\log\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785{{\log_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\log_{\mathchar 28724}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\over\log_{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785}}\mathchar 314

1.1.
Organization

In Section
6
, we use the Chinese Remainder theorem alongside estimates for smooth algebraic integers to reduce Theorem
1.1
to Theorem
6.5
. In Section
5
we utilize the Landau–Page theorem for number fields to obtain a version of the Bombieri–Vinogradov theorem for number fields with a strong error term, for use in Section
7
. The bulk of this paper, in Section
7
, is devoted to the proof of Theorem
7.9
, a number field variant of the uniform estimates for prime
\mathchar 29035
-tuples in
[
10
]
. We use this to deduce Theorem
7.10
, which gives the existence of a sieve weight analogous to sieve weight defined in
[
3
]
*Section 7. In Section
9
, we define the probability weight used to prove Theorem
6.5
, and using Theorem
7.10
we deduce Corollary
9.3
. Finally, combining Corollary
9.3
with Theorem
10.1
(which is a corollary of the hypergraph covering theorem in
[
3
]
*Theorem 3), we deduce Theorem
6.5
.

Acknowledgements

The author thanks Kevin Ford, Jesse Thorner, and Gergely Harcos for many helpful comments and corrections.

2.
Notational conventions

Throughout this paper we adopt the following typographical convention:

(1)

Ideals
of the ring of integers
\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}
are denoted by
fraktur letters, e.g.,
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29025}\mathchar 24891\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}\mathchar 12826\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}
.

(2)

Algebraic integers
(elements of
\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}
) are written in
Dutch calligraphic letters, e.g.
\dc\mathchar 29025\mathchar 24891\dc\mathchar 29026\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}
.

(3)

Rational integers
(elements of
\mathbb{\mathchar 29018}
) are denoted by the default TeX math font, e.g.,
\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29041\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29018}
.

The implied constants in this paper may depend on the imaginary quadratic field
\mathchar 29003
in an unspecified manner. We write
\mathchar 29030\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29007_{\mathchar 12820}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29031\delimiter 84054785
if
\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29030\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29031
. We write
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29025}\vartriangleleft\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}
to mean that
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29025}
is an integral (i.e., not fractional) ideal of
\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}
.
Define

\mathchar 28953_{\mathchar 28999}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\#\{\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}\vartriangleleft\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 12346\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}\text{ prime, }\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29048\}\mathchar 314

For an ideal
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29025}\vartriangleleft\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}
, let
\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathfrak{\mathchar 29025}\delimiter 84054785
denote the ideal norm
\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29003\delimiter 68408078\mathbb{\mathchar 29009}}\delimiter 67273472\mathfrak{\mathchar 29025}\delimiter 84054785
of the ideal
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29025}
. As usual, we define
\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29003}
for algebraic integers
\mathchar 29025\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}
by
\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29025\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29025\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785
. For
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29025}\vartriangleleft\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}
, define

\mathchar 28675\delimiter 67273472\mathfrak{\mathchar 29025}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\begin{cases}\log(N_K(\mf{p}))&\mathfrak{\mathchar 29025}\mathchar 12349\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}^{\mathchar 29035}\\
\mathchar 28720&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We define the Möbius function
\mathchar 28950
on prime power ideals by
\mathchar 28950\delimiter 67273472\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721
and
\mathchar 28950\delimiter 67273472\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
for
\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28722
. We extend
\mathchar 28950
to all prime ideals
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29025}\vartriangleleft\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}

For
\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29018}

\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 4945\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 29040\mathchar 314

For
\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29018}
, we write
\mathchar 29008^{\mathchar 8235}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785
and
\mathchar 29008^{\mathchar 8704}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785
to denote the largest and smallest prime factors of
\mathchar 29038
respectively, with the conventions that
\mathchar 29008^{\mathchar 8235}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
and
\mathchar 29008^{\mathchar 8704}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 561
.

Whenever we use the variable
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29041}\vartriangleleft\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}
, we assume that
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29041}
is relatively prime to the difference between any two units in
\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}

\mathchar 29007\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785

\mathfrak{\mathchar 29041}

Finally, we write

3.
Preliminaries

In this section, we record several standard results for later use. First, we require Landau’s prime ideal theorem, in the following form (from
[
12
]
*Theorem 8.9):

Theorem 3.1

(Landau)
.

Let
\mathchar 29003
be an algebraic number field of finite degree over
\mathbb{\mathchar 29009}
, and let
\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}
denote the ring of algebraic integers in
\mathchar 29003
. Then for
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28722

\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}
of
\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}
with

\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29048

is

\#\{\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}\vartriangleleft\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}_{\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 12346\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29048\}\;\mathchar 12349\;\mathrm{\mathchar 29004\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785\;\mathchar 8235\;\mathchar 29007_{\mathchar 29003}\bigl\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\exp(-c\sqrt{\log x})\bigr\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

where
\mathchar 29027\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720
is a constant depending on
\mathchar 29003
.

4.
Ray classes in
\mathchar 29003
and Hecke
\mathchar 29004
-functions

In this section, we define the notion of ray classes in the number field
\mathchar 29003
(which generalize arithmetic progressions over the integers), and Hecke
\mathchar 29004

\mathchar 29004

Lemma 5.1

or else
\mathchar 29044\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720

6.

Lemma 7.2
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29037}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12906\dc{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29034}\dc{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 8235\dc{\mathchar 29026}_{\mathchar 29034}
for every
\mathchar 29034
and
\mathbf{\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 12850\mathscr{\mathchar 28997}
. Then
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29037}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathfrak{\mathchar 29037}_{\mathchar 29035}
are pairwise relatively prime, and
\left\delimiter 67273472\mathfrak{\mathchar 29037}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\dc{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
for all
\mathchar 29034
.

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}\mathchar 12906\mathfrak{\mathchar 29037}_{\mathchar 29033}
and
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}\mathchar 12906\mathfrak{\mathchar 29037}_{\mathchar 29034}
then
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}
divides
\dc{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29033}\left\delimiter 67273472\dc{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29034}\dc{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 8235\dc{\mathchar 29026}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\dc{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29034}\left\delimiter 67273472\dc{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29033}\dc{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 8235\dc{\mathchar 29026}_{\mathchar 29033}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\dc{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29033}\dc{\mathchar 29026}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 8704\dc{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29034}\dc{\mathchar 29026}_{\mathchar 29033}
, and so
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 28997
. This proves the first claim. Since
\left\delimiter 67273472\dc{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 28721}\dc{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 8235\dc{\mathchar 29026}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\dc{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\dc{\mathchar 29026}_{\mathchar 29035}\right\delimiter 84054785
is an admissible set,
\left\delimiter 67273472\dc{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\dc{\mathchar 29026}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
for all
\mathchar 29034
. Hence, if
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}
is prime,
\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}\left\delimiter 69640972\mathfrak{\mathchar 29037}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 69640972\left\delimiter 67273472\dc{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29034}\dc{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 8235\dc{\mathchar 29026}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 84054785
and

\mathfrak{\mathchar 29040}\mathchar 12906\dc{\mathchar 29026}_{\mathchar 29034}

\left\delimiter 67273472\dc{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathfrak{\mathchar 29037}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
.
∎
